## Page 1


AFFINE SPRINGER FIBER AND THE SMALL QUANTUM GROUP
ROMAN BEZRUKAVNIKOV, PABLO BOIXEDA ALVAREZ, MICHAEL MCBREEN, AND ZHIWEI YUN
Abstract. We find a new geometric incarnation for the principal block in the category of modules over
a quantum group at a root of unity, realizing it as a full subcategory of microsheaves on a certain affine
Springer fiber. We also prove a related geometric Langlands type equivalence with wild ramification,
identifying the latter category with a category of coherent sheaves on the Springer resolution for the dual
group. This can also be viewed as a version of homological mirror symmetry for the Springer resolution.

1. Introduction
The results of this article have several sources of motivation belonging, respectively, to representation
theory, geometric Langlands duality and homological mirror symmetry.
From the representation theory perspective, the main result (see Theorem 1.1.4) is a new geometric
realization of a category of modules over the quantum group at a root of unity.
Earlier theorems of
this nature identified a category of quantum group modules with perverse sheaves on the affine or semi-
infinite flag manifold, which implies the relation between the appropriate version of the affine Kazhdan-
Lusztig polynomials to numerical invariants of representations.
Theorem 1.1.4 below relates quantum
group modules to a different geometric object: it asserts its equivalence with a full subcategory Pψ of
microsheaves on a certain affine Springer fiber Flψ, where ψ = tψ1 ∈g((t)) and ψ1 ∈g is regular semisimple;
or more precisely, microsheaves on a certain symplectic variety Mψ supported on a conical Lagrangian
subset homeomorphic to Flψ. The known and expected applications (see [23] and Theorem 1.3.2 below)
are also different.
The category Pψ of microsheaves on Flψ also admits a description in terms of perverse sheaves: it turns
out to be equivalent to a full subcategory in the category of perverse sheaves on the affine flag variety
Fl of G, Whittaker-equivariant with respect to the first congruence subgroup G[t−1]1 in the group of
negative loops G[t−1] equipped with the additive character given by ψ; the space Mψ above is the shifted

Hamiltonian reduction of T ∗Fl by the action of G[t−1]1 (by a shifted Hamiltonian reduction we understand
the quotient of the preimage of dψ = ψ1dt under the moment map). We believe that the microlocal
description of the category of (partially) Whittaker sheaves is a general phenomenon with strong potential
applications in representation theory, see 1.4.4 below. To make sense of Whittaker sheaves in an arbitrary
sheaf-theoretic context (without necessarily the Artin-Schreier sheaves or exponential D-modules at our
disposal), we use the notion of Kirillov category introduced by Gaitsgory [26].
The quotient of the affine flag variety by the congruence subgroup G[t−1]1 is the moduli stack of G-
bundles on P1 with level structures at zero and infinity, so the study of sheaves on this space belongs in
geometric Langlands duality. Accordingly, we establish an equivalence (see Theorem 1.1.3) between the
derived category of microsheaves and the derived category of coherent sheaves on a stack of Langlands
parameters which can be identified with the derived category of quantum group modules based on [2] and
[48].
On the other hand, we can also realize the symplectic variety Mψ as the moduli space of Higgs bundles
on P1 with a regular singularities at zero and an irregular one at infinity (of order 2), as explained in [9].
There is a corresponding Betti space MBet,ψ, diffeomorphic to a quotient of Mψ by an action of the lattice
X∗(T) of cocharacters of a maximal torus T ⊂G. The space MBet,ψ is (for suitable choices of parameters)
a smooth affine variety of finite type, and thus comes equipped with a contractible space of Weinstein
structures. Microsheaves on MBet,ψ may be identified with a version of the Fukaya category, using the
general theory developed in [29]. One expects that the category of microsheaves on Mψ is equivalent to
a suitable X∗(T)-graded version of the Fukaya category of MBet,ψ.
Combining the above discussions with the geometric Langlands type equivalence in Theorem 1.1.3,
one would thus establish homological mirror symmetry (HMS) between MBet,ψ and a space of Langlands
parameters. While this case of HMS was among our sources of motivation, we do not work with Fukaya
categories in this paper, thus this statement remains conjectural.
1.1. Kirillov category and microsheaves on the affine Springer fiber.
1.1.1. Affine Springer fiber. Let G be a connected reductive group over C with Lie algebra g. Let C((t))
be the field of Laurent series in one variable t, and let G((t)) be the associated loop group with Lie algebra
g((t)) = g ⊗C C((t)).
Let I ⊂G((t)) be an Iwahori subgroup.
Let ψ1 ∈g be regular semisimple and
ψ = tψ1 ∈g((t)). We denote by Flψ = {g ∈G((t))/I|Ad(g−1ψ ∈Lie I)} the affine Springer fiber of ψ in the
affine flag variety Fl = G((t))/I. Note that Flψ has irreducible components in bijection with the extended
affine Weyl group f
W = X∗(T) ⋊W, and each component has dimension equal to that of the flag variety
B of G (in fact some components can be identified with B). It also carries a Gm-action given by loop
rotation (scaling t).
Just as the usual Springer fibers, the affine Springer fiber Flψ can be embedded into a symplectic variety
Mψ as a Lagrangian. Moreover, the Gm-action on Flψ extends to Mψ, and all points of Mψ contracts to
points in Flψ under this action. This is not a general structure for any affine Springer fiber but crucially
relies on the Gm-symmetry on Flψ. A more general construction of this kind is done in our previous paper
[9].
1.1.2. Kirillov category. In geometric representation theory we often encounter sheaf-theoretic versions of
Whittaker models whose definition involves an Artin-Schreier sheaf on the affine line, so a priori only makes
sense over a characteristic p base field, or in the context of D-modules where we replace Artin-Schreier sheaf
by the exponential D-module. Gaitsgory [26] gives a way to define such geometric Whittaker categories in
any sheaf-theoretic context (in particular in the Betti setting), when the Ga-action on the space involved
extends to an action of Ga ⋊Gm (where Gm acts on Ga by scaling). We apply his construction 1 to the
moduli stack BunG(Gψ

1.2. Microsheaves and microlocal fiber functor for Satake category. As mentioned in the begin-

1.3.3. Cohomology of affine Springer fiber and center of small quantum group. Another application is to
the study of the center of small quantum group, see [35] and [10]. The main result of [10] is an injective
homomorphism (conjecturally an isomorphism) from H∗(Flψ) to the center (endomorphisms of the identity
functor) of ub0
q−modgr.
Theorem 1.1.4 provides a new way to construct and study this homomorphism. Namely, we have a
natural map from H∗(Flψ) to endomorphisms of the identity functor in µShFlψ,fs(Mψ) preserving the
homological degree. Using the purity of H∗(Flψ) one can check that Koszul duality sends this action to an
action by degree zero endomorphisms of the Koszul dual triangulated category resulting in a homomor-
phism H∗(Flψ) →End(Idub0
q−modgr). We plan to develop this application elsewhere.
1.4. Further directions.
1.4.1. Coherent description of all microsheaves on Flψ. We expect Theorem 1.1.3 to extend to a full
embedding from the larger category µShFlψ(Mψ) to the category of coherent sheaves on a Betti moduli
space for G∨, a special case of the Betti moduli spaces introduced in [40], see also [9, Section 4].

1.4.4. Microsheaves on usual Springer fibers. Let e be a nilpotent element in g, Se ⊂N be the (nilpotent
part of the) Slodowy slice through e and eSe be the preimage of Se under the Springer resolution. Then
the Springer fiber Be is a conical Lagrangian in eSe, and we may consider the category of microsheaves
µShBe(eSe). Our construction in this paper suggests a sheaf-theoretic model for µShBe(eSe), namely a full
subcategory in the category Kir(Ve\B), where Ve is the kernel of the character ψe : Ue →Ga for a Premet
subgroup Ue and its character ψe whose differential equals the linear functional defined by e. For example,
if e is even and {e, h, f} is an sl2-triple, then Lie Ue is the sum of negative weight spaces of g under h. We
expect this category to be equivalent to the category of finite dimensional modules over the corresponding
W-algebra with an integral regular central character. Notice that this category is closely connected to
the D-modules (or Harish-Chandra bimodules) realization of the asymptotic Hecke category [37] and to
character D-modules [13]. We expect the above topological realization to yield a generalization of these
results to the constructible setting.
1.4.5. Characteristic p version. We expect that our results can be extended to the setting when the
coefficient field E has positive characteristic. The analogue of Theorem 1.1.4 is a Koszul duality between
the category of microsheaves and a regular block in the category of (G∨
1 , T ∨)-modules where G∨
1 is the
kernel of the Frobenius morphism for the reductive group G∨over E. Notice that a key ingredient in
the proof of Theorem 1.1.4, the relation of modules over the Lusztig quantum group and perverse sheaves
on the affine Grassmannian [2] has a positive characteristic analogue known as the Finkelberg–Mirkovi´c
conjecture, which was established in [18] with some restrictions on char(E). However, the proof of Theorem
1.1.4 relies also on the formality from weights argument which is not available in positive characteristic,
thus the generalization requires a different technical implementation of the strategy outlined in Remark
8.1.2.

1.4.6. Coherent duality for affine Springer fibers. We briefly describe a heuristic relation of our results and
the more general [9, Conjecture 1.4.1] to the conjectural equivalence between two coherent categorifications
of the double affine Hecke algebra (DAHA), which is a local counterpart of the classical limit of the
geometric Langlands duality.
Varagnolo and Vasserot [49, Theorem 2.5.6] introduced a categorification of DAHA coming from the
derived category of equivariant coherent sheaves on the loop group analogue of the Steinberg variety (a
similar construction for the spherical, degenerate DAHA appeared in [12]). One expects that the monoidal
categories constructed this way from two Langlands dual group G, G∨are naturally equivalent, see [12,
§7.9] for the similar conjecture in the spherical case. Next, this equivalence of monoidal categories should
come with compatible equivalence of module categories as follows.
We fix an invariant quadratic form yielding a vector space isomorphism t ∼= t∨and an isomorphism
of Chevalley spaces t/W ∼= t∨/W. Let e ∈γ((t)) and e∨∈γ∨((t)) be regular semisimple topologically
nilpotent elements whose images in the Chevalley space match under this isomorphism. Then one expects
an equivalence
DCoh(Fle) ∼= DCohZe∨(Fle∨)
where Ze∨is the centralizer of e∨and Fle, Fle∨are the affine Springer fibers; (1.4.6) should be an
equivalence of module categories over the two coherent categorifications of DAHA.
The heuristic of S1-localization proposed by Ben-Zvi and Nadler [5] suggests that (1.4.6) should imply
another equivalence of categories obtained, respectively, from the left hand side by quantization and from
the right hand side by passing to the similar object for the finite dimensional group G∨replacing the
corresponding loop group. In the special case when e = ts as above, the two categories can be arguable
identified with the two sided of the equivalence in Theorem 1.1.3.
1.5. Notation and convention.
1.5.1. Sheaf-theoretic contexts. Let X be an algebraic stack of finite type over an algebraically closed field
k. Let E be a field of characteristic zero depending on the sheaf-theoretic context. We will use the notation
D(X) to denote any of the following E-linear sheaf-theoretic categories in different contexts:
• ´Etale: take E = Qℓwhere char(k) ̸= ℓ. Let Db(X, E) be the bounded derived category of Qℓ-
complexes of sheaves for the ´etale topology of X. We will reserve the notation D(X) for a variant
of Db(X, E) by formally adding a half Tate twist as in §1.5.2.
• Betti: k = C and E is any field of characteristic zero field. We take D(X) to be the bounded
derived category of constructible sheaves on X with E-coefficients for the classical topology on
X(C).
• De Rham: char(k) = 0 and E = k. We take D(X) to be the bounded derived category of algebraic
D-modules on X.
The main body of the paper is written in the ´etale context, although all arguments in the paper work for
the Betti and de Rham contexts equally well.

2. Kirillov category
We elaborate on Gaitsgory’s construction of the Kirillov category, which will serve as a model in the
usual sheaf-theoretic context for talking about microsheaves. We also give an alternative construction of
the Kirillov category in §2.3 that is more flexible for applications.
We will freely use material about monodromic sheaves such as monodromic vanishing cycles and mon-
odromic Fourier transform, which are reviewed and further developed in Appendix A.
2.1. Kirillov category after Gaitsgory [26]. Let Aff = Ga ⋊Gm be the group of affine transformations
of A1. Let X be a stack with an action of Aff.
2.1.1. Definition. The Kirillov category for the stack X with Aff-action is the quotient
Kir(X) = DGm(X)/DAff(X)
under the forgetful functor DAff(X) ,→DGm(X).
Denote the quotient functor by κ : DGm(X) →Kir(X).
Let Kir∗(X) ⊂DGm(X) be the kernel of the averaging functor
AvGa,∗: DGm(X) →DAff(X).
In other words, Kir∗(X) is the right orthogonal to the full subcategory DAff(X) ⊂DGm(X). Let κ∗:
Kir∗(X) ,→DGm(X) be the inclusion, then κ◦κ∗: Kir∗(X) →Kir(X) is an equivalence. The composition
Kir(X) ∼= Kir∗(X)
κ∗

2.3. Alternative construction. We give an alternative construction of the Kirillov category that makes
certain symmetries more transparent. The setup is the same as §2.1. This construction is only used in the
rest of paper to make sense of integral transforms on Kirillov categories given by kernel sheaves that are
Ga-equivariant and Gm-monodromic (as opposed to Gm-equivariant), see §2.3.9. Such integral transforms
will allow us to define Hecke symmetries on automorphic Kirillov categories (see §6.4 and §7.1.4).
In this subsection alone, we work with cocomplete versions of sheaf categories (ind-constructible sheaves).
2.3.1. Monodromic category. Let DGm-mon(X) ⊂D(X) be the full subcategory generated by the image
of DGm(X) under the forgetful functor, i.e., unipotently monodromic complexes. By Verdier [50, Section
5], every object F ∈DGm-mon(X) carries a unipotent action of the tame fundamental group πt
1(Gm)
(unipotent means a topological generator acts unipotently). Moreover, all morphisms in DGm-mon(X) are
πt
1(Gm)-equivariant. In other words, the identity functor of DGm-mon(X) carries a unipotent action by
πt
1(Gm).
Let GE = Spf
\

1(Gm)], where the completion is with respect to powers of the augmentation ideal of
the group algebra E[πt
1(Gm)]. With a choice of a topological generator of πt
1(Gm), GE is isomorphic to the
formal multiplicative group over E. The category of ind-coherent sheaves IndCoh(GE) is by construction
equivalent to complexes of E-vector spaces with a unipotent action of πt
1(Gm), under which convolution

as
T ∗X //1 Ga := µ−1
Ga(1)/Ga.
2.4.1. Definition. The relevant locus for the Ga-action on X is the image of the projection T ∗X //1 Ga =
µ−1
Ga(1) →X (a constructible substack of X). By abuse of language we also call the image of the projection
T ∗X //1 Ga →X/Ga the relevant locus. A geometric point in the relevant locus of X is called a relevant
point; otherwise it is called irrelevant.

2.4.2. Lemma. A geometric point x ∈X(K) (where K is an algebraically closed field) is relevant if and
only if the homomorphism
(2.18)
AutX/Ga(x) →Ga,K
is trivial, i.e., the stabilizer of x under Ga is trivial.
Equivalently, x is irrelevant if and only if the homomorphism (2.18) is nonzero, i.e., Ga fixes x.
Proof. By definition, x is relevant if and only if the restriction of the moment map µGa to its cotangent
fiber µGa,x : T ∗
xX →(Lie Ga)K is nonzero. Dually, x is relevant if and only if the infinitesimal action map
(Lie Ga)K →TxX is nonzero (here TxX is the degree zero cohomology of the tangent complex of X at
x). This happens if and only if the stabilizer of x under Ga has 0 Lie algebra, which means the stabilizer
is trivial.
□
2.4.3. Lemma. Let i : Y ,→X be a locally closed Aff-stable smooth substacks of X such that all geometric
points in X\Y are irrelevant. Then
(1) Both functors i! : Kir(Y ) →Kir(X) and i∗: Kir(Y ) →Kir(X) are equivalences.
(2) The natural transformation i! →i∗: Kir(Y ) →Kir(X) is an isomorphism.
Proof. Decomposing i as a closed embedding followed by an open embedding, we are reduced to treat the
case where i is either a closed embedding or an open embedding. The proof for both cases follows along
the same lines, so in the following assume we are in either of the two cases.
Now let X′ = X −Y , which consists of irrelevant points. We first show that Kir(X′) = 0. Indeed,
let F ∈DGm(X′). Since all points in U are irrelevant, by (2.4.2), all points in U are fixed by Ga. This
implies that F is Ga-equivariant (which is a property since Ga is contractible), hence its Gm-equivariant
structure extends uniquely to an Aff-equivariant structure, which implies F becomes zero in Kir(X′) =
DGm(X′)/DAff(X′).
We prove (2).
For F ∈DGm(Y ), we have a distinguished triangle i!F →i∗F →k∗k∗i∗F where
k : X′ ,→X. Since k∗i∗F ∈Kir(X′) = 0, we see that i!F →i∗F becomes an isomorphism in Kir(X).
We prove (1). Since i! and i∗are full embeddings, it suffices to show that they are essentially surjective.
For F ∈DGm(X), we have a distinguished triangle i!i!F →F →k∗k∗F. Since k∗F ∈Kir(X′) = 0, we
conclude that i!i!F →F becomes an isomorphism in Kir(X). The same argument shows that F →i∗F
becomes an isomorphism in Kir(X).
□
2.4.4. Corollary. Suppose {Yα}α∈I is a finite collection of disjoint, locally closed Aff-stable smooth sub-
stacks of X.
Assume there exists an Aff-invariant stratification X = ⊔α∈IXα such that Yα ⊂Xα
and all geometric points in Xα\Yα are irrelevant (here by stratification we mean each Xα is locally
closed and smooth, I has a partial order such that Xα = ∪β≤αXβ for all α ∈I).
Then the images
of iα! : Kir(Yα) →Kir(X) generate Kir(X), and Kir(X) admits a semi-orthogonal decomposition into
{Kir(Yα)}α∈I.
Proof. Let jα : Xα ,→X be the inclusion. Since {Xα}α∈I form an Aff-invariant stratification of X, the
images of jα! : Kir(Xα) →Kir(X) generate Kir(X), and Kir(X) admits a semi-orthogonal decomposition
into {Kir(Xα)}α∈I. By Lemma 2.4.3, the !-extension functors Kir(Yα) →Kir(Xα) are equivalences, hence
the same statements are true if Kir(Xα) is replaced with Kir(Yα) for each α ∈I.
□
3. Recollections on microsheaves
In this section we assume the base field k = C, and work with the analytic topology on various smooth
complex varieties. We will review the basics of microsheaves, and recall a few properties which we will
have use for later, as well as some general context and motivation. None of this material is original.
3.1. Microsheaves.


## Page 16


16
ROMAN BEZRUKAVNIKOV, PABLO BOIXEDA ALVAREZ, MICHAEL MCBREEN, AND ZHIWEI YUN
3.1.1. Microsheaves on cotangent bundles. We follow [32] and [42] to recall the formalism of microsheaves.
Let N be a smooth complex manifold, and let Sh(N) be the dg category of unbounded complexes of
sheaves on N. For any subset Λ ⊂T ∗N, let ShΛ(N) be the full subcategory of sheaves with singular
support contained in Λ. For an open subset Ω⊂T ∗N (with respect to the usual topology on a complex
manifold), let Sh(N; Ω) be the quotient Sh(N)/ShT ∗N−Ω(N). The assignment µShpre : Ω7→Sh(N; Ω) is
a presheaf of stable ∞-categories. Let Ω7→µSh(Ω) be the sheafification of µShpre. It is a sheaf of stable
∞-categories.
For any open Ω⊂T ∗N, an object F ∈µSh(Ω) has a well-defined singular support SS(F), which is a
conical subset in Ω. For a subset Λ ⊂T ∗N and open Ω⊂T ∗N, we let µShΛ(Ω) denote the category of
sections of µSh(Ω) with singular support in Λ ∩Ω.
For F, G ∈Sh(N), in [32] a complex of sheaves µhom(F, G) on T ∗N is defined. We have
HomµSh(Ω)(F, G) = H0(Ω, µhom(F, G)).
The support of µhom(F, G) is contained in SS(F) ∩SS(G).
3.1.2. Extension to stacks. We refer to [43, Section 4] for more details on microsheaves on a stack. We
will only need the case of a global quotient X = [N/G] where N is a smooth scheme over C. In this case,
T ∗X = [µ−1(0)/G] where µ : T ∗N →g∗is the moment map for the G-action on N. For Ω⊂µ−1(0) that
is G-invariant, we can still form µShpre(Ω/G) := Sh(X)/Shµ−1(0)−Ω(X).
3.1.3. Definition. Define a sheaf of stable ∞-categories µSh over the stack T ∗X by sheafifying the above
presheaf. We denote its global section category by µSh(T ∗X) = µSh(µ−1(0)/G).
3.1.4. Microsheaves on exact symplectic manifolds. An exact sympectic manifold is a (smooth, real) man-
ifold X equipped with a real one-form λ, such that ω = dλ is a symplectic form. The image of λ under
the isomorphism of vector bundles T ∗X →TX defined by ω is called the Liouville vector-field. The flow
of this vector field is called the Liouville flow.
3.1.5. Example. Let N be a manifold, and let X = T ∗N be equipped with the usual symplectic structure.
The action of R>0 dilating the cotangent fibers is the Liouville flow for the canonical Liouville form, which
we denote λN.
Exact symplectic manifolds bear a close relation to contact manifolds. To an exact symplectic manifold
(X, λ), we associate the contact manifold (Y, α) = (X × R, λ + dt).2
We will consider contact manifolds (Y, α) and symplectic manifolds (X, ω) equipped with an extra
structure called Maslov data. This structure endows them with sheaves of categories locally modeled on
the microsheaf categories defined by by Kashiwara and Schapira in [32]. We briefly sketch the definition
of Maslov data below; a good reference for this material is [21, Section 3].
3.1.6. Remark. We will use the same notation µSh, and the same word microsheaves, for the sheaves of
categories on a contact or symplectic manifold. As we recall below, there is a simple relationship between
the two, and in practice this should not cause any confusion.
Let C be a stable presentable symmetric monoidal category (eventually, this will be the category of
coefficients for our microsheaves, and we may take it to be dg Z-modules or dg C-vector spaces).
3.1.7. Definition. Let (X, ω) be a real symplectic manifold. There is a canonical sequence of maps
(3.1)
X →B U →B(U / O) →B2 Pic(C)
where the first map classifies the stable tangent bundle to X (viewed as a symplectic bundle), the second
map is the delooping of the quotient map U →U / O, and the last map is induced by a de-looping of the
J-homomorphism. Maslov data for (X, λ) is a choice of null-homotopy of the resulting map X →B2 Pic(C).
We will denote such a choice of nullhomotopy by the variable η below.
2One can also build a symplectic manifold from a contact manifold, though we will not need to do so here.

3.1.8. Example. When C = Z −mod, B2 Pic(C) is given by B2(Z) ⊕B3(Z/2Z). A choice of Maslov data
is canonically identified with a choice of trivialization of the complex line bundle det(TCX)⊗2 and a class
in H2(X, Z/2Z).
Maslov data over other commutative rings R can then be obtained by composition with Pic(Z−mod) →
Pic(R −mod).
Maslov data for contact manifolds is defined similarly to the symplectic case, where the contact distri-
bution plays the role of the tangent bundle. In particular, Maslov data for an exact symplectic manifold
(X, λ) determines Maslov data for (X × R, λ + dt).
One natural way of producing Maslov data is as follows.
3.1.9. Definition. A stable polarization of X is a section of the Lagrangian Grassmanian bundle LGr(TX⊕
R2n) →X for some n ≥0.
We consider stable polarizations up to homotopy: they are classified by null-homotopies of the compo-
sition
(3.2)
X →B U →B(U / O)
of the first two maps in Equation 3.1.
A choice of stable polarization defines Maslov data for X by
composition.
3.1.10. Example. If X = T ∗N is a cotangent bundle, then it carries a natural polarization, and thus
natural Maslov data, which we denote ηN. Similarly, if Y = S∗N is the cosphere bundle of a manifold N,
equipped with the canonical contact form αN, then it carries a natural (contact) polarization, and thus
natural Maslov data, which we also denote ηN.
The papers [47] and [42, Theorem 1.1] endow any contact manifold (Y, α) equipped with a choice of
Maslov data (Y, α, η) with a sheaf of categories µSh, which we call microsheaves.
Suppose (Y, α, η) = (S∗Rn, αN, ηN). Then the stalk of this sheaf at p ∈S∗Rn is the category
(3.3)
Sh(Rn)/ShS∗\p(Rn),
where Sh(Rn) denotes sheaves on Rn with coefficients in C. This is essentially the category defined by
Kashiwara and Schapira in [32], except that one works here with coefficients in a presentable category,
rather than with bounded complexes.
The same is true, non-canonically, for general (Y, α, η). Any homotopy of Maslov data η →η′ induces
an isomorphism on microsheaves. On the other hand, by the contact version of Darboux’s theorem, (Y, α)
is locally isomorphic, as a contact manifold, to the cosphere bundle S∗Rn of a vector space. Thus the
sheaf µSh is always locally isomorphic to (3.3).
3.1.11. Definition. Given an exact symplectic manifold equipped with a choice of Maslov data (X, λ, η),
we consider the associated contact manifold (X ×R, λ+dt, η) equipped with the induced Maslov data. We
then define µSh on X as the pullback of µSh on X×R along the natural embedding i0 : X →X×0 ⊂X×R.
We call an object of µSh(X) a microsheaf.
3.1.12. Proposition. The support of a microsheaf is a closed coisotropic subset of X stable under the
Liouville flow.
Given any subset S ⊂X, we can consider the subsheaf µShS ⊂µSh of objects supported on S.
3.1.13. Remark. One could also pull back µSh on X × R along the graph of any function f : X →R; this
corresponds to shifting the Liouville form by df.
3.1.14. Remark. The dependence of µSh on the Maslov data η is mild: any isotopy η →η′ induces an
equivalence on microsheaves. On the other hand, the stalks of µSh can depend quite non-trivially on the
form λ.
One of the main results of [42] is that in good situations, the global sections of µSh along the so-called
core c(X, λ) ⊂X are invariant with respect to (sufficiently nice) exact isotopies of λ.

3.1.15. Proposition. Any isomorphism of (X, λ, η) with an open subset Ω⊂(T ∗N, λN, ηN) identifies µSh
with the sheaf defined in Section 3.1.1.
3.1.16. Comparing two constructions of microsheaves. Suppose a Lie group G acts on a manifold N,
preserving an open subset Ω⊂T ∗N, and let X = Ω//θG = µ−1(θ)/G be a symplectic reduction at some
moment value θ ∈(g∗)G. The cotangent polarization on T ∗N restricts to Ω.
The pullback π∗TX is the middle cohomology of the sequence g →TΩ→g∗defined by the group
action and moment map. As explained by Nadler and Shende in [43], any choice of G-equivariant splitting
TΩ∼= π∗TX ⊕g ⊕g∗identifies G-equivariant stable polarizations of Ωand stable polarizations of the
quotient.
3.1.17. Proposition. [43] Let (W, λ) be a complex exact symplectic manifold, which occurs as an open
subset of T ∗X = T ∗(X/G). Endow W with the quotient polarization, with associated Maslov data η. Then
Definition 3.1.11) and Definition 3.1.3 agree after restriction to W.
3.1.18. Objects from smooth lagrangians.
3.1.19. Definition. Let (X, λ, η) be an exact symplectic manifold equipped with Maslov data. Let L ⊂X
be a smooth Lagrangian submanifold, and let Ω∼= T ∗L be a Weinstein neighborhood of L. Secondary
Maslov data for L is a choice of homotopy from the cotangent Maslov data on T ∗L to the restriction η|Ω.
3.1.20. Proposition. [42] Any Lagrangian submanifold L ⊂X such that λ|L = 0, equipped with secondary
Maslov data, defines a fully faithful functor Loc(L) →µSh(X).
The condition λ|L = 0 can be relaxed to λ|L = df under certain conditions : see Theorem 3.2.4.
3.2. Microsheaves and Fukaya categories. In this section, we recall the relationship between mi-
crosheaves and the Fukaya category. This material is motivational, and will not be used anywhere else in
this paper.
An exact symplectic manifold X is called Liouville if the Liouville flow is complete, and X is convex,
in the sense that it admits an exhaustion {Xk ⊂X|k ∈N} by compact submanifolds with boundaries,
such that the Liouville vector field is outward pointing along each boundary. We call such a pair (Xk, λ)
a Liouville domain.
3.2.1. Definition. The core c(X, λ) ⊂X (sometimes called the skeleton) of a Liouville manifold (X, λ) is
the maximal flow-invariant subset of Xk (for k ≫0) which is disjoint from ∂Xk.
This does not depend on the choice of exhaustion. The core is a compact subset of X.
3.2.2. Proposition. Any compactly supported object in µSh(X) is supported in c(X, λ).
3.2.3. Definition. Let Sh(X, λ) = µShc(X,λ)(X, λ).
A Liouville form λ is sufficiently Weinstein if c(X, λ) is sufficiently isotropic [43, Definition 9.10]. This
holds, for example, if it is Whitney-stratified by isotropic submanifolds. There is a corresponding notion
of sufficiently Weinstein homotopies between sufficiently Weinstein Liouville manifolds.
It is proven in [43] that a sufficiently Weinstein homotopy λ →λ′ induces an equivalence of Sh(X, λ)
with Sh(X, λ′). One consequence of this is the following.
3.2.4. Theorem. [43, Theorem 1.2] Let L be a compact exact Lagrangian submanifold of the sufficiently
Weinstein manifold (X, λ, η), equipped with secondary Maslov data.
There is a fully faithful functor
Loc(L) →Sh(X, λ).
3.2.5. Theorem. [29, Theorem 1.4] Let (X, λ, η) be a Liouville manifold admitting homological cocores.3
Let Sh(X, λ)c denote the category of compact objects. Then there is a canonical equivalence of dg categories
Sh(X, λ)c ∼= Perf W(X, λ)op
where Perf W(X, λ) is the (opposite) category of the idempotent-completed pre-triangulated closure of the
wrapped Fukaya category of X.
3We refer to [29] for a definition of homological cocores.

4. Affine Springer fiber and Hitchin moduli space
Let k be an algebraically closed field.
4.1. Homogeneous affine Springer fiber with slope one. Let G be a connected reductive group over
k with Lie algebra g and flag variety B. Let ψ1 ∈g be a regular semisimple element. Then T = CG(ψ1)
is a maximal torus. Let Φ = Φ(G, T) be the set of roots of G with respect to T, and W the Weyl group.
For α ∈Φ, let gα ⊂g be the corresponding root space.
Let G((t)) be the loop group of G with formal parameter t. Let G = G[[t]] be the formal arc group of G.
In other words, G((t))(R) = G(R((t))) and G(R) = G(R[[t]]) for k-algebras R. Let Gr = G((t))/G be the
affine Grassmannian of G.
Let B ⊂G be a Borel subgroup containing T, and I ⊂G the corresponding Iwahori subgroup. Then
Fl = G((t))/I is the affine flag variety of G. Let Φ+ ⊂Φ be the subset of positive roots defined by B.
Write ψ = tψ1 ∈g((t)). This element defines its affine Springer fiber Flψ in the affine flag variety Fl
and Grψ in the affine Grassmannian Gr as in [33]:
Flψ = {g ∈Fl = G((t))/I0|Ad(g−1)(ψ) ∈Lie I0};
Grψ = {g ∈Gr = G((t))/G|Ad(g−1)(ψ) ∈g[[t]]}.
Using the terminology of [45, §3.1], the element ψ is a homogeneous element of slope 1 in the loop Lie
algebra g((t)).
4.1.1. Geometric properties. By the dimension formula of Kazhdan-Lusztig-Bezrukavnikov [33] and [6],
we have
dim Flψ = dim Grψ = |Φ+| = dim B.
Moreover, Flψ and Grψ are known to be equidimensional.
Now B = G/B ⊂Fl is contained in Flψ,
therefore it is an irreducible component of Flψ.
4.1.2. Symmetry. Since ψ has centralizer T((t)) in G((t)), T((t)) acts on Flψ and Grψ by left translation. In
particular, we get a natural action of the lattice X∗(T) on Flψ and Grψ: λ ∈X∗(T) acts by left translation
by tλ.
4.1.3. Hessenberg varieties. Let Grot

be the one-dimension torus acting on G((t)), Fl, Gr and g∗((t)) by

m preserves Flψ and Grψ. We describe the Grot

The fixed points of Grot

of n copies of P1 intersecting over a unique point over 0 (it is still locally planar at the intersection).

Aψ is contracting to aψ.
The basic geometric properties of Mψ is summarized below.
4.3.3. Proposition ([9, Theorem 2.8.1, Lemma 2.15.6]).
(1) The stack Mψ is a smooth algebraic space
locally of finite type over k of dimension |Φ| (the number of roots of G).
(2) The space Mψ carries a canonical symplectic structure with weight 1 under the Gm-action.
(3) The map fψ is a completely integrable system. In particular, fibers of fψ are Lagrangians in Mψ.
(4) For any ξ ∈Mψ, the limit point lims→0 s · ξ under the Gm-action exists.

Liouville form λ.

4.7.1. Definition. Let µSh be the sheaf of stable ∞-categories on Mψ whose restriction to Mi
ψ is defined
as above.
4.7.2. Proposition. Any compactly supported object in µSh(Mψ, λ) is supported on a finite union of
irreducible components of the central Hitchin fiber Λψ ⊂Mψ.
Proof. The support of any such object is conical with respect to the Liouville flow. Any compact conical
subset must be contained in Λψ.
Since the support is coisotropic, it must be a union of irreducible
components of Λψ.
□

ROMAN BEZRUKAVNIKOV, PABLO BOIXEDA ALVAREZ, MICHAEL MCBREEN, AND ZHIWEI YUN
For this and other reasons (see the discussion of Fukaya categories below), it is natural to restrict our
attention to the subcategory µShΛψ(Mψ, λ) of microlocal sheaves supported on Λψ. We also sometimes
abuse notation and write this as µShFlψ(Mψ, λ).
4.7.3. Lattice action. The action of the lattice X∗(T) on Mψ does not commute with the action of C∗,
and in particular does not preserve the one-form λ and does not map conical subsets of Mψ to conical
subsets. For the same reason, the lattice does not act on the category µSh(Mψ, λ). However, X∗(T) does
preserve the subset Λψ, and we expect that it acts on µShΛψ(Mψ, λ) by automorphisms. Indeed, for each
σ ∈X∗(T), one may pick a homotopy between σ∗λ and λ such that Λψ is fiberwise conical. We expect
that these homotopies, composed with the translation action of σ ∈X∗(T) induce an action of X∗(T) on
µShΛψ(Mψ, λ), following the construction in [42]. We will not use this action in the present paper. It
should be the microlocal counterpart of the action defined in Section 6.2.
4.8. Speculations on Fukaya categories. We now turn to the question of associating a Fukaya category
to the space Mψ. The form λ does not make Mψ into a Liouville manifold, because Mψ is not of finite
type. There are various methods available to try and circumvent this problem; we sketch two approaches
here.
4.8.1. Via localization to the core. Recall the Hitchin map f : Mψ →Aψ. Fix a linear metric on Aψ, and
consider the family of balls Bk ⊂Aψ of radius k > 0 centered at aψ. Set Mk
ψ = f −1(Bk) ⊂Mψ. Then
the flow of the vector field associated to λ is outward pointing along ∂Mk
ψ. The core of (Mψ, λ) may be
defined as in Definition 3.2.1, where we set Xk = Mk
ψ.
4.8.2. Lemma. The core of Mψ is the central fiber of the Hitchin fibration: c(Mψ, λ) = Λψ.
Proof. The Liouville vector field dilates Aψ, with unique fixed point aψ. Hence c(Mψ, λ) ⊂f −1(aψ) = Λψ.
The opposite containment follows from the fact that Λψ is a union of compact conical subsets.
□
The fact that the core Λψ is ind-compact is a clue that (Mψ, λ) in many respects behaves like a finite
type Liouville manifold. For instance, the statement of Theorem 3.2.4 should hold for (Mψ, λ). We may
then seek to define the Fukaya category in terms of Sh(X, λ) := µShΛψ(Mψ, λ), converting Theorem 3.2.5
into a definition.
4.8.3. Via the lattice quotient. A natural approach to producing a (finite type) Liouville manifold from
Mψ is to take a quotient by the action of the lattice X∗(T), which preserves the symplectic form and the
subset Λψ. The lattice action is free and discontinuous on Λψ ⊂Mψ, and the quotient Λψ/X∗(T) is of
finite type. The same should be true of a small tubular neighborhood of Λψ.
4.8.4. Definition. Fix a small ball Bϵ ⊂Aψ centered at aψ, and let Yψ = f −1(Bϵ)/X∗(T).
The space Yψ is a finite type manifold, which we wish to endow with a Liouville structure.4 As mentioned
above, the action of X∗(T) on Mψ does not preserve the Liouville form λ, and hence the latter does not
descend to the quotient.
One can instead try to deform λ to something X∗(T)-invariant as follows. Suppose we are given a
real-valued function h : Mψ →R, such that for all σ ∈X∗(T) we have σ∗dh = dh + θσ. Here θσ = ιVσω
and Vσ is the vector field generating the action of Re(C∗) defined by σ.
Then λh = λ + df will be a X∗(T)-invariant Liouville form, which descends to Yψ. One should further
require that the associated isotopy of Liouville vector fields preserves Λψ and dilates Aψ. The result would
then be a Liouville manifold (Yψ, λh) whose Fukaya category can be locally modelled by µShΛψ(Mψ, λ).
We do not at present know how to find such a function h. However, its existence would follow from the
existence of a suitable hyperk¨ahler structure on X by the same construction as in [28].
In this case, one
4The space Yψ is a smooth affine complex variety and thus a Stein manifold.
It therefore carries a natural family
of Weinstein structures. It is difficult, however, to establish a direct relation between these Wenstein structures and the
Liouville structure on Mψ defined above. For the same reason, it is difficult to relate the Fukaya categories defined from
these Weinstein structures to the categories considered in this paper, although we expect a close such relation exists.

5. Affine Hecke category with level (G1
∞, ψ)
In this section we study the structure of versions of the affine Hecke category with deeper level structure
(G1
∞, ψ). They will act on the categories Dψ,G and Dψ, the main players in §6 and §7. A more general
type of affine Hecke categories were studied by Kamgarpour–Schedler [31] in the ℓ-adic context over a
positive characteristic base field. Our contribution here is: (1) we work with the Kirillov model so that
the results hold in any sheaf-theoretic context; (2) for our purposes we need to work with a completed
monodromic version of the affine Hecke category in the spirit of [19, Appendix A].

Similarly, by considering the multiplication of the second and third factors in Fλ,µ,ν we get a map
m23 : Fλ,µ,ν →Fλ,µ+ν whose fibers are isomorphic to Fµ,ν. The map zβ can be similarly written as a
composition, from which we see that it is also given by capping with the fundamental class of Fλ,µ,ν. This
shows that zα = zβ and finishes the proof.
□

6. The category Dψ,G
In this section we study the category Dψ,G that carries commuting actions of H∞and the Satake
category Hsph
0

we construct a fiber functor on the Satake category using microstalks, categorifying an old result of Evans
and Mirkovic [22].

7. The category Dψ
We introduce and study the main player of this paper, a conventional sheaf category on the affine flag
variety that turns out to be equivalent to microsheaves on the affine Springer fiber Flψ with finite type
supports.

7.4.1. Example (SLn). We consider the pushforward of F0 to Dψ,P for certain parahorics P and consider
some irreducible summands of these when G = SLn.
The only cases of interest are when s0 ∈WP. Let P′ be the parahoric whose Weyl group is given by
W ∩WP, and let P ⊂G be the corresponding parabolic subgroup. Then the map πP factors through πP′.
Thus we can simply compute the pushforward along the map πP′,P.
Assume that WP includes si for i < k and i > j and does not include sj and sk. Then the relevant
orbits in G/P →BunG(G1
∞, P) are given by
HP(0) = {(Vi) ∈G/P|ψVk ⊂Vj}.
This is a Hessenberg variety in G/P, generalizing the variety H(0) from Section 4.1.3. Our category
thus contains π∗
PEHP(0)[dim(HP(0) + dim(P/B)], the pullback of the constant sheaf on the Hessenberg
variety HP(0), as one of the irreducible objects.

However, making it into a rigorous argument involves some subtleties of homotopical algebra, including a

rings over a given commutative ring. The reduction is achieved by invoking Koszul duality; as a byproduct
of this approach we get a stronger statement involving an equivalence of abelian categories.

Appendix A. Specialization and microlocalization
In this appendix we review the Kashiwara-Schapira microlocalization construction in the algebro-
geometric context over an algebraically closed field of any characteristic.
We also prove formulas for
computing the specialization and microlocalization in the situation with a contracting Gm-action.
A.1. Specialization.
A.1.1. Monodromic sheaves. Let X be a stack with Gm-action. Following Verdier [50, Def.3.1 and §5],
an object F ∈D(X) is called monodromic if for any x ∈X with the action map ax : Gm →X (sending
t ∈Gm to t · x), the pullback a∗
xF has tame locally constant cohomology sheaves. Let Dmon(X) ⊂D(X)
or DGm-mon(X) ⊂D(X) denote the full subcategory of monodromic objects.

→π1!(F|M1).
Proof. By the fact that Ψp commutes with Verdier duality, it suffices to prove the first isomorphism in
(A.20). By the contracting assumption, Z = M Gm ⊂M0. Let D be the henselization of A1 at 0. Let
j : Mη →MD = M ×A1 D. Let i : M0 ,→MD be the inclusion. By definition, Ψp(F) = i∗j∗Fη. Let
πZ : Z →N be the restriction of π. Using the contracting principle for the Gm-monodromic sheaf ΨpF
on M0, we see
(A.21)
π0∗Ψp(F)
∼
→πZ∗i∗
ZΨp(F) ∼= πZ∗((j∗Fη)|Z).
Let πD : MD →N be induced from π. Apply the contraction principle to the Gm-monodromic sheaf j∗Fη
on MD, we have a canonical isomorphism
(A.22)
πη∗Fη = πD∗j∗Fη
∼
→πZ∗((j∗Fη)|Z)
under the identification D(N) ∼= D(Nη). Combining (A.21) and (A.22) we get the desired isomorphism
(A.20).
□
